## management-governance — module synthesis

# Management-Governance Module — UBER (Synthesis)

## Abstract

Uber's management is competent but only partly aligned with shareholders. The CEO, nine years in the seat, has a clean, checkable record of beating its own profit guidance, and capital spent building the core business earns strong returns — but capital spent buying other companies has mostly failed (two of the two largest deals with observable outcomes, Transplace and Drizly, did not deliver), and a $14.8 billion bet on Delivery Hero, funded mostly with new debt, is still pending. Executive pay cannot be assessed because the 2026 proxy statement is missing from the data pool, and insiders own only about 0.18% of the company with no open-market cash buying identified in six months. The single biggest flag: Uber quietly stopped publishing the reconciliation behind the profit number it keeps beating. Verdict: misaligned-to-weak stewardship — competent operators, not clearly aligned with minority shareholders.

## 1. Stewardship Verdict

- **Verdict: Misaligned or weak stewardship** — thin insider ownership with no cash buying, a value-destructive-to-date M&A record (capped by the §24 serial-acquirer filter), and mixed candor (a discontinued profit reconciliation) outweigh a genuinely strong operating and guidance-delivery record and a structurally clean board.
- **Hard disqualifier flagged (business-model/01)?** N — `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` tested all 8 disqualifiers (audit qualification, pledging >50%, RPT >25%, auditor churn, restatement, enforcement, customer concentration, negative OCF) and found none triggered; no verdict lock applies from that scan.
- Management quality /100: **74**
- Capital allocation /100: **50** *(capped from an unweighted 58 by RF-CAP-004, the §24 Filter 4 serial-acquirer cap)*
- Incentive alignment /100: **5** *(Insufficient Data — hard cap is max 50, non-binding; the constraint is the missing proxy, not the cap ceiling)*
- Shareholder friendliness /100: **60** *(capped from a blended ~62 — Board & Shareholder Rights 72, Ownership Alignment 52 — by the missing-ownership-data cap)*
- Disclosure candor /100: **51**
- Governance risk /100 *(higher = worse)*: **66**
- Data quality /100: **62** *(from 00 — most governance inputs present; the DEF 14A proxy, the single highest-value document, is genuinely absent)*
- Overall usefulness /100: **65** *(capped at max 70 by the missing-proxy rule)*
- Insider ownership (one line): ~0.18% of shares outstanding held by officers/directors; CEO holds 0.060% ($87.8mm); nearly all "purchases" are $0-value RSU vests, not cash buys; net insider selling over 3 and 6 months (modest, scheduled-looking) [04].
- Biggest governance signal (one line): Uber discontinued its consolidated GAAP-to-Adjusted-EBITDA reconciliation starting Q1 FY2026 while continuing to report quantified guidance beats on that exact metric — the company's most-cited profitability number can no longer be independently checked against GAAP from the primary filing [06, RF-DISC-001].
- **Governance Score /100** = 0.20×50 (CapAlloc) + 0.18×5 (Incentive) + 0.18×60 (ShFriendliness) + 0.16×51 (Candor) + 0.16×74 (MgmtQuality) + 0.12×(100−66) (GovRisk) = 10.0 + 0.9 + 10.8 + 8.16 + 11.84 + 4.08 = **45.8 ≈ 46**
- **Confidence-Adjusted Governance Score /100** = 46 × 58/100 = **27**
- **Governance Rating**: **Weak** (40–54 band; consistent with the Critical-flag rule requiring no better than Weak — satisfied without needing to force it)
- **Confidence Score /100** (source quality): **58** — audited-filing and board data are Tier-1/high-confidence, but the comp/ownership-percentage/AGM-vote/certificate-of-incorporation facts rest on web-sourced aggregation (confidence 2–3) because the primary proxy is absent
- **Red-Flag Count / Critical Red-Flag Count:** **7 / 3**

## 1A. Module Disconfirmation

- **Strongest bear point:** The serial-acquirer pattern is not hypothetical — of the two largest deals with observable post-close results, Transplace/Uber Freight ($2.25bn, FY2021) has posted three straight years of revenue decline and stayed loss-making, and Drizly (~$1.1bn, FY2021) was shut down entirely within three years — and the team is now committing $14.8bn (funded mostly by a new ~€14.2bn bridge facility that roughly doubles total debt) to Delivery Hero, a company Uber already competes with, while insiders hold 0.18% of the stock and made no identifiable open-market cash purchase in six months [02, 04].
- **Strongest bull point (steelman):** The CEO has a nine-year, non-founder tenure built on a real, delivered turnaround (first positive free cash flow in 2022, sustained GAAP profitability since), a checkable four-for-four Adjusted EBITDA guidance-beat streak through Q2 FY2026, a 90%-independent board with a split chair/CEO and fully independent committees, no promoter, no dual-class stock, and a contractual pledging ban — genuinely clean structural governance, not decoration [01, 05].
- **Single killer risk:** The pending $14.8bn Delivery Hero acquisition repeating the Transplace/Drizly value-destruction pattern while roughly doubling debt — precisely as the company's headline profitability metric has become harder to independently verify because the GAAP reconciliation behind it was discontinued [02, 06].
- **Disconfirming evidence already visible:** FY2025 buybacks reduced net share count for the first time in the record (−1.9%), and incremental ROIC on the organic platform since profitability was reached (FY2023→FY2025) computes to roughly 24.6% — real evidence that capital kept inside the core business, as opposed to M&A, has been per-share value-creative [02].

## 3. Reconciliation

Two disagreements surface across the specialists, both resolved conservatively:

1. **Strong operating execution (01: 74/100) vs. capped capital allocation (02: 50/100).** Agent 01 documents a genuinely strong, checkable promise-vs-delivery record on the metric management actually guides. Agent 02 shows that the same management team's M&A record — the mechanism by which it is now deploying its largest sums of capital ($14.8bn on Delivery Hero) — has been value-destructive on the only two large deals with observable post-close results. Reconciled view: operating competence and capital-allocation discipline are not the same skill, and this module treats them separately rather than letting the strong operating score launder the weak M&A record. The capital-allocation score stands at its capped 50, not blended upward.

2. **Structurally clean board (05: 72/100) vs. thin ownership and no incentive visibility (03/04: Insufficient Data / 52/100).** Agent 05 finds a well-formed governance box — 90% independent, split chair/CEO, no promoter, no pledging. Agents 03 and 04 find that the people this board oversees have almost no personal capital riding on the stock (0.18% insider ownership, no identifiable cash buying) and that the incentive design cannot be checked at all because the proxy is missing. Reconciled view: procedural independence does not substitute for evidenced alignment — a board can be structurally sound and still oversee a management team whose comp cannot be verified as per-share-value-linked. Shareholder friendliness is capped at 60, not credited with 05's uncapped 72.

**Forensic tag propagation (CLAUDE.md §13; eval check AQ).** `06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md` fired all three of the following as standalone lines, propagated here: **RF-DISC-001** (management continued to make quantified "beat guidance" claims on an EBITDA-type metric whose GAAP reconciliation the company stopped publishing starting Q1 FY2026 — Critical materiality) and **RF-DISC-002** (a "legal, non-income tax, and regulatory reserve changes and settlements" line recurred at $1,123M FY2024 / $564M FY2025 despite its "adjustment" framing — High materiality), and **RF-REG-002** (two adverse MDL jury verdicts, Feb-2026 and Apr-2026, were not named in the three consecutive filings that followed them — Medium materiality). These three tags should feed the master synthesizer's cross-module forensic roll-up alongside `earnings/06`'s RF-EQ-001/RF-EQ-002 and `balance-sheet-survival/05`'s RF-OBS-001 to check for a compounding accounting-integrity mosaic.

## 5. Stewardship Summary

Yes and no. Management has delivered on the specific promises it makes — four straight quarters of Adjusted EBITDA beaten against its own guided range, two of them above the guided high end by a near-identical ~2.5% — but that record does not extend to capital allocation: of the two largest acquisitions with observable post-close results, both failed (Transplace/Uber Freight is smaller and still loss-making four years and $2.25bn later; Drizly was shut down within three years of its ~$1.1bn purchase), and the team is now committing $14.8bn to Delivery Hero, funded mostly by new debt, following the same acquisitive pattern. Incentives cannot be assessed at all — the 10-K defers every comp detail to a 2026 proxy statement that is not in the data pool — so it is not possible to say whether pay rewards per-share value or size, and that uncertainty must be read conservatively, not as a clean bill of health. Insiders own a rounding error of the company (0.18%) and made no identifiable open-market cash purchase in six months, putting essentially no personal capital behind the stated conviction. Minority shareholders sit inside a structurally clean board (90% independent, split chair/CEO, no promoter, no pledging) that nonetheless holds every procedural lever — no special meetings, no written consent, no cumulative voting — and is now the subject of an unresolved derivative lawsuit alleging it ignored an internal risk tool for years. The single most important reason for caution: a management team with a real, delivered operating turnaround behind it is about to make its largest-ever capital bet using the exact playbook (debt-funded, competitor-overlapping M&A) that has not worked the two other times it was tried at scale, while the number it is guided and judged against (Adjusted EBITDA) recently became harder for outsiders to independently verify.

## 7. Note To The Final Synthesizer

- **Stewardship verdict: Misaligned or weak stewardship.** Strongest supporting evidence: a §24 Filter-4-tripped serial-acquirer capital-allocation pattern (RF-CAP-004) — Transplace ($2.25bn) and Drizly (~$1.1bn), the only two large deals with observable post-close results, both failed — combined with thin insider ownership (0.18%, no identifiable cash buying) and a discontinued profit reconciliation (RF-DISC-001).
- **Capital-allocation record: mixed, capped at 50/100.** Organic reinvestment is genuinely value-creative (~24.6% incremental ROIC, FY2023→FY2025; capex held to 6.4% of cumulative CFO) and FY2025 buybacks reduced net share count for the first time. M&A has been value-destructive on the observable evidence, and the pending $14.8bn Delivery Hero deal — funded mostly by ~€14.2bn of new debt and requiring a $1.6bn forced divestiture — is the largest, least-proven capital-allocation decision in the record.
- **Incentives and ownership: cannot confirm alignment with minority holders.** Comp metrics/weights are Insufficient Data (2026 proxy missing from pool); insiders hold 0.18% of the company with zero identifiable open-market cash purchases in six months and modest net selling. Do not read this absence of red flags as a clean bill of health — it is a genuine unknown, not a clearance.
- **Biggest governance risk / red flag: RF-DISC-001 (Critical) — Uber discontinued its consolidated GAAP-to-Adjusted-EBITDA reconciliation starting Q1 FY2026 while continuing to claim quantified guidance beats on that exact metric**, which `earnings/08` independently found leaves a $734mm (35.2%) unbridgeable gap. Close second: an unresolved, unadjudicated shareholder derivative lawsuit (filed Jul-23-2026) naming the entire board over an alleged sexual-assault-reporting discrepancy (12,522 disclosed vs. 400,181 alleged, 2017-2022).
- **Hard disqualifier flagged by `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan`: None.** All 8 disqualifier tests returned N; no verdict lock applies. The routed integrity "buzz" (driver-classification litigation; legacy 2016-breach FTC/DOJ matters) was investigated and cleared as pre-Khosrowshahi / structurally unrelated to current management — RF-MGT-005 was NOT triggered.
- **§24 rejector filters tripped:** Filter 4 (serial acquirer, RF-CAP-004) — CapAlloc capped 50, GovRisk floored 60. Filters 1 (integrity), 2 (turnaround), and 6 (unaligned owner) were tested and NOT tripped — see Section 4 for the evidence on each.
- **Partial-data caps applied:** Incentive alignment (Insufficient Data; hard cap max 50, non-binding since the actual evidence-based score is 5); Shareholder friendliness (capped at 60 from a blended ~62, on the missing ownership/insider-transaction data); Overall usefulness (capped at 70, actual 65). No caps applied to management quality, capital allocation's base methodology, board disclosure, or disclosure candor (each had sufficient primary evidence, independent of the RF-CAP-004 conviction cap already applied to capital allocation).
- **Biggest missing data point:** The 2026 DEF 14A Proxy Statement — it would simultaneously resolve the compensation-metric, beneficial-ownership-percentage, insider-transaction-history, and RPT-policy gaps, and is the single document every relevant 10-K item (11-14) points to by reference.
- **Explicit handoff:** This module supersedes the `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance` quick-read. The master synthesizer should treat THIS module's governance verdict (Misaligned or weak stewardship), Governance Score (46/100), Confidence-Adjusted Score (27/100), and Governance Rating (Weak) as the primary governance read for UBER.

## 8. Simple Summary

- Management keeps its word on the one number it guides — Adjusted EBITDA has beaten guidance four quarters running, twice above the high end.
- Money spent building the actual business earns strong returns (~24.6% incremental return since 2023); money spent buying other companies has mostly failed — Transplace and Drizly, the two big deals with visible results, both did not work out.
- Executive pay cannot be checked at all — the document that would show it (the 2026 proxy) is missing from the data pool.
- Insiders own about 0.18% of the company and have not bought any stock with their own cash in the last six months — they are net sellers, though modestly and on a schedule that looks routine.
- Minority shareholders sit behind a genuinely independent-looking board (90% independent, split chair/CEO) that nonetheless holds every procedural lever (no special meetings, no written consent, no cumulative voting) and is now the target of an unresolved lawsuit alleging it missed internal warning signs for years.
- Uber quietly stopped publishing the math behind the profit number it keeps beating — a real candor concern, even though nothing checked against a primary filing was found to be false.
- No hard governance disqualifier was flagged — no fraud finding, no pledging, no runaway related-party dealing, no auditor issue.
- This module is useful but incomplete for the master synthesizer: the board, capital-allocation, and candor reads are solid; the compensation and precise-ownership reads are not assessable until the 2026 proxy is added to the data pool.

RF-REG-002,Delayed / undisclosed material litigation naming,Medium,"Two federal-jury MDL verdicts (Arizona $8.5mm Feb-2026; Charlotte NC $5000 Apr-2026) not named in 3 most recent filings; aggregate reserve disclosed",FY25 10-K; Q1/Q2 FY26 10-Q; CIQ Key Developments 2026-08-08,Disclosure Candor component -8,Check next 10-Q/10-K for MDL naming
UNREGISTERED-BOARD-OVERSIGHT,Shareholder derivative lawsuit naming full board,Critical (materiality; confidence 2/5 unadjudicated),"Filed Jul-23-2026 (N.D.Cal.); names CEO Chairman all 8 other sitting directors president and 2 former CFOs; alleges board ignored 2017-built ML risk model until 2022 while disclosed incident counts (12522 2017-2022) diverged from alleged internal total of 400181",Web Forbes 2026-07-23,Board & Shareholder Rights component -10,Track docket for motions to dismiss discovery or settlement

## 6. Sufficiency Verdict

- **Verdict:** Partial
- **Reason:** Board composition, related-party figures, control structure, multi-year capital-allocation history, management-turnover events, and two earnings-call transcripts are all present and usable, but the proxy/DEF 14A that would carry individual executive-compensation tables (metrics and weights), the beneficial-ownership percentage table, and Section 16 insider-transaction data is genuinely absent from the pool — a true gap for a US SEC filer, not a jurisdiction-mapping error.
- **Specialists that can run:** management track record (01), capital allocation (02), board and shareholder rights (05, on the data that is present), candor (06) — all with the pool's own filings, transcripts, and the CIQ dossiers. Incentives (03) and ownership/insider behavior (04) can run but are capped per MODULE_RULES.md (see below) because their primary source document is missing.
- **Hard disqualifier already flagged by business-model/01_disqualifier-scan?** N — no disqualifier triggered (all 8 tests: N). A non-lock "integrity buzz" note was routed from that scan: two federal-jury sexual-assault liability verdicts (Charlotte, NC — $5,000 damages; Arizona — $8.5 million damages) tied to Uber's Safety Risk Assessed Dispatch algorithm and driver-classification litigation are disclosed, ongoing/resolved legal matters, not proven fraud by the controller or senior management, and do not touch financial-reporting integrity — flagged here for the candor and management-track-record agents to weigh as context (RF-MGT-005 territory to evaluate), not as a verdict-lock trigger.
- **Active partial-data caps:**
  - No proxy / compensation disclosure → Incentive alignment (03) max 50; Overall usefulness (99) max 70.
  - No ownership / insider-transaction data → Shareholder friendliness (04/99 read) max 60.
- **Critical missing items:**
  - 2026 DEF 14A Proxy Statement (executive compensation tables and metric weights, beneficial-ownership percentage table, Section 16 insider-transaction history, RPT policy narrative, AGM vote-result tallies) — not in `data/UBER/`.
  - Standalone 8-K filings for the CFO transition (Feb-2026) and CPO/President reorganization (May-2026) — only available secondhand via the CIQ Key Developments vendor feed, not the primary SEC filing.
- **Single highest-value missing document:** The 2026 DEF 14A Proxy Statement (or, failing that, the prior-year DEF 14A) — it would resolve the compensation-metric, beneficial-ownership, and insider-transaction gaps simultaneously and is the single document Items 10–14 of the FY25 10-K point to by reference.

# Management & Track Record — UBER

## 1. Key Executives

| Role | Name | Appointed | Tenure | Relevant Background | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| CEO & Director | Dara Khosrowshahi | September 2017 | ~9 years | Former CEO/President of Expedia Group (Aug 2005–Aug 2017); earlier CFO of IAC/InterActiveCorp; recruited to Uber after the Travis Kalanick-era culture and legal scandals (workplace conduct, the 2016 data-breach cover-up, Waymo trade-secrets suit) to professionalize the company ahead of its 2019 IPO. Sits on the boards of Expedia Group and Hotels.com; independent-director roles at Aurora Innovation and Grab Holdings both ended in the past 12 months (Dec-2024 and Jul-2026 respectively). | [CIQ Professionals export, Khosrowshahi entry]; [FY25 10-K, Item 3 / Note 14 — Driver Classification and legacy legal matters, context] |

MacDonald | June 2, 2025 (Web: press reports, unverified date; pool confirms the prior-role end date) | ~1.2 years in this role; joined Uber 2012 (~14 years at the company) | Ran Uber's global Mobility business as SVP, Head of Global Mobility (2019–Jun 2025); held regional GM roles 2012–2019; ex-Bain & Company consultant. First person to hold the COO title at Uber since 2019 — media coverage at the time of appointment framed it as CEO Khosrowshahi "staying put" rather than grooming an imminent successor. | [CIQ Professionals export, MacDonald entry: "Senior Vice President, Head of Global Mobility from 2019 to June 2025"]; [Web: Bloomberg, "Uber Promotes Company Veteran to COO...", 2025-06-02 (unverified, dated)] |

Non-executive, separate from the CEO role. | [CIQ Board Members export, Sugar entry: "Years on Board: 2018-Present"; "Chairman of Corporate Governance Committee"] |

Travis Kalanick, the other co-founder and former CEO, is off the board entirely and had fully divested his Uber stake by 2023 [FY25 business-model synthesis, capital-allocation-governance doc]. | [CIQ Board Members export, Camp entry: "Board Observer and Product Advisor of Uber Technologies, Inc. from May 11, 2020 and served as its Director until May 11, 2020"] |

**Founder / owner-operator status.** Uber is not run by a founder or an owner-operator. Both co-founders (Kalanick, Camp) are gone from executive and voting-board roles; Kalanick fully divested his stake by 2023 [business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md]. The CEO is a professional outside hire (Khosrowshahi, ex-Expedia) brought in nine years ago specifically to replace founder-era leadership after governance and conduct failures. There is no controlling shareholder or promoter group — Uber has been widely held since its 2019 IPO, with 1,207 holders of record as of Feb 10, 2026 [FY25 10-K, Item 5, p.5289, cited in business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md]. This is a professionally managed, board-governed company, not a founder-led one — alignment therefore has to be read through incentive design and insider buying (agents 03/04), not through founder skin-in-the-game.

## 2. Management Stability

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Senior departures (last 3 years) | Two C-suite-level departures within a 3-month window in 2026: CFO Prashanth Mahendra-Rajah stepped down Feb 16, 2026 after ~2.3 years in the role (appointed Nov 2023 per web-sourced press coverage — unverified, dated 2023-09/2023-11) and was replaced same-day by internal candidate Balaji Krishnamurthy; Chief People Officer Nikki Krishnamurthy stepped down May 11, 2026, with her responsibilities absorbed into a newly created "President & Chief Corporate Affairs Officer" role for existing SVP Jill Hazelbaker (an internal reshuffle, not an external hire). Both departing executives were retained in advisory/transition capacities (Mahendra-Rajah as Senior Finance Advisor through Jul 1, 2026; Nikki Krishnamurthy as an advisor through a transition period), and the company stated the CFO change "is not the result of any disagreements concerning financial disclosures or accounting matters." | [CIQ Key Developments, "Uber Technologies, Inc. Announces Chief Financial Officer Changes, Effective February 16, 2026"]; [CIQ Key Developments, "Uber Technologies, Inc Announces Executive Changes, Effective May 11, 2026"] |
| CFO turnover | Uber has had three CFOs since September 2018: Nelson Chai (Sept 2018 – Jan 2024, ~5.3 years, departure announced Aug 2023, credited by the CEO with shepherding the 2019 IPO and the company's first positive-free-cash-flow quarter in Aug 2022 — web-sourced, unverified, dated); Prashanth Mahendra-Rajah (Nov 2023 – Feb 16, 2026, ~2.3 years); Balaji Krishnamurthy (Feb 16, 2026 – present, ~6 months as of this report). The current CFO's tenure is too short to independently assess; the prior transition was orderly (advance notice, transition advisory role, explicit no-accounting-dispute statement) rather than abrupt. Two CFO changes in roughly 8 years is not an extreme pattern for a company of this size and age, but it is worth monitoring given the CPO departure landed in the same 3-month window. | [Web: Bloomberg/PYMNTS/CFO Dive coverage of Chai and Mahendra-Rajah appointments/departures, 2023 (unverified, dated) — cross-checked against the pool-sourced Feb-16-2026 CFO transition, which is the only CFO change directly evidenced in this data pool] |
| CEO succession / continuity | No CEO change. Khosrowshahi has led Uber continuously since September 2017 (~9 years) through the IPO, the COVID-19 demand collapse, the shift to sustained GAAP profitability (FY2025 net income $10,053mm on revenue $52,017mm), and now the pending Delivery Hero acquisition. The June 2025 creation of a President & COO role (first time since 2019) adds a layer of depth below the CEO but was publicly framed as the CEO "staying put," not a handover signal. | [CIQ Professionals export, Khosrowshahi entry: "Chief Executive Officer and Director of Uber Technologies, Inc. since September 2017"]; [CIQ Key Developments, Q4 FY25 earnings entry, Feb-04-2026: FY2025 net income $10,053mm, revenue $52,017mm]; [Web: Bloomberg, 2025-06-02, unverified] |

**Read on stability.** The CEO seat is stable and long-tenured — a genuine positive. The finance seat has turned over twice in roughly eight years, and the CFO and Chief People Officer changes landing three months apart in 2026 is a cluster worth flagging, even though both replacements were internal promotions rather than a scramble to hire externally, and the company was explicit that the CFO exit was not accounting-related. This is an Amber signal — monitor, not a red flag.

## 3. Stated Strategy & Targets

Management describes the current phase as extending "the proven strategy that has underpinned our growth for years" along two fronts: (1) geographic and category expansion — most concretely the pending acquisition of a further 63.21% of Delivery Hero (bringing Uber's economic interest toward 100%) for approximately $14.8 billion, which management says will "expand our reach to nearly 100 markets" by roughly doubling the number of markets where Uber can run its full mobility-plus-delivery platform; and (2) autonomous vehicles, where the CEO's stated ambition is "to become the world's leading commercialization platform for autonomous vehicles" by partnering with multiple AV technology providers (Waymo, Wayve, Zoox, and others) rather than building the technology itself [Q2 FY26 transcript, prepared remarks — Dara Khosrowshahi]. On the numbers side, Uber's only current formal, checkable quarterly guidance covers Adjusted EBITDA and (since Q1 FY26) EPS Normalized; it does not guide revenue, capex, or free cash flow as ranges [earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md]. CEO commentary at the Q2 FY26 print pointed to Gross Bookings growth above 20% for a fourth consecutive quarter and trailing-12-month free cash flow exceeding $10 billion for the first time — offered as evidence the growth-plus-margin-expansion strategy is working, not as a forward numeric target [Q2 FY26 transcript, prepared remarks].

**Read on promise vs delivery.** On the one metric Uber has guided continuously and specifically (Adjusted EBITDA), the record over the last four reported quarters is a clean sweep — beaten in all four, and beaten above the guided high end in the last two by a near-identical ~2.5% margin each time [earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md]. The newer EPS Normalized guide (one quarter of history) was also met. Revenue, which Uber does not guide, has run slightly below consensus the last two quarters, but at least one of those misses is explained by a disclosed accounting reclassification (the UK Mobility change) rather than a genuine demand shortfall. This is a strong, checkable record on the metrics management actually commits to — not a vague "beat the Street" narrative.

## 4A. Turnaround & Integrity Tests (CLAUDE.md §24, Filters 1 & 2)

| Test | Finding | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Is the thesis a turnaround (struggling business + new management / new plan)? | No. Uber is a mature, profitable, growing platform business under a nine-year-tenured CEO; current strategy is expansion (Delivery Hero acquisition, AV commercialization) from a position of strength (TTM free cash flow >$10bn), not a rescue of a distressed operation. The genuine Uber turnaround — cleaning up the Kalanick-era culture, executing the 2019 IPO, and reaching sustained profitability — happened under this same CEO between 2017 and roughly 2022–2023 and has already been delivered, not merely promised: first positive free cash flow quarter (Aug 2022, web-sourced), first GAAP-profitable full year (FY2023), and now four straight quarters of EBITDA-guidance beats. That is a completed turnaround, not a live one requiring a base-rate discount today. | [FY25 10-K, Item 7 MD&A financial results]; [Web: Uber Q2 2022 earnings commentary re: first positive FCF quarter, unverified, dated]; [earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md] |
| If yes: years of *proven* operating inflection actually delivered (not promised) | Not applicable in the present-tense sense (see above); if scored as the historical 2017–2023 governance/profitability turnaround, it clears the ≥2–3 year delivered-improvement bar comfortably (FY2022 first positive FCF quarter through FY2025's $10,053mm net income and four straight quarters of EBITDA-guidance beats through Q2 FY26 is 3–4+ years of demonstrated, not promised, improvement). | [earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md, Sec. 6] |
| Résumé / plan / consultant deck cited as the reason to believe? (not evidence) | No live turnaround pitch is being made here; the Delivery Hero deal and AV-commercialization ambition are growth/capital-allocation bets layered on an already-profitable base, evaluated separately in `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` (which caps the Capital Allocation score at 50/100 for the serial-acquirer pattern — Filter 4, not Filter 2). | [business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md, Sec. 4] |
| Routed integrity "buzz" from `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` (unverified adverse signal) | The disqualifier scan routed two items as non-lock context: driver-classification litigation (an industry-wide labor-classification dispute, not an allegation against a named executive) and a 2018 FTC privacy consent decree plus a 2022 DOJ non-prosecution agreement tied to Uber's 2016 data-breach cover-up. Both regulatory matters predate the current CEO's tenure in substance — the breach and its cover-up occurred under founder-era leadership before Khosrowshahi's September 2017 arrival, and his hiring was itself part of the board's response to that era's governance failures. Assessed as disclosed, resolved-or-ongoing legal/regulatory exposure attributable to a leadership team no longer in place, not an unresolved adverse signal about current management's integrity. **No `RF-MGT-005` is emitted** — this does not meet the bar of an unresolved, unproven adverse signal about the people currently running the company; it is investigated and read as context (largely favorable context, in that it explains why the current CEO was brought in) rather than left as an open flag. | [business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md, Sec. 3, "Non-lock note on integrity"]; [FY25 10-K, p.4348 (FTC/DOJ historical matters)] |

**Turnaround rule applied.** No conviction cap from Filter 2 applies — there is no live turnaround thesis to discount. **Integrity rule applied.** The routed signal was chased against the pool's own timeline evidence (CEO start date vs. incident dates) and read as resolved/contextual rather than left open; no RF-MGT-005 is triggered.

## 5. Management Read

The CEO seat has been stable for nine years under a professional operator brought in specifically to clean up founder-era governance failures, and the record since then — IPO execution, the 2022 shift to positive free cash flow, and now four consecutive quarters of Adjusted EBITDA guidance beaten (two of them above the guided high end by a near-identical margin) — is a checkable pattern of promises kept, not a narrative. The CFO seat has turned over twice in about eight years, most recently in February 2026, and a second senior departure (the Chief People Officer) landed three months later; both changes were internal promotions handled with advance notice and explicit statements ruling out accounting disputes, so this reads as a stability signal to monitor rather than a red flag. The single biggest management signal, positive or negative, is the now-pending $14.8 billion Delivery Hero acquisition: it is being pursued by a management team with a genuinely strong operating and guidance-delivery record, but it is also the clearest test yet of whether that same team allocates capital as disciplined as it operates — a question this agent does not score (see `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md`, which already caps the capital-allocation score for the serial-acquirer pattern) but that materially conditions how much credit the operating track record above should be given going forward.

## management-governance / 02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md

_Source: `02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md`_

# Capital-Allocation Scorecard — UBER

Reporting currency: USD (millions, except per-share and per-unit figures). Window covered: FY2021–FY2025 (5 fiscal years, Dec-31 year-end), with the trailing-twelve-months (TTM) through Jun-30-2026 and the pending Delivery Hero deal (agreed Jul-16-2026) shown as forward-looking context. Uber is a US SEC filer (10-K / 10-Q); no jurisdiction-mapping adjustment is needed [FY25 10-K, p.1].

## 1. Uses of Capital (FY2021–FY2025)

| Use | Cumulative Amount | Currency | % of cumulative CFO ($21,018mm, FY21–25) | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---|
| Acquisitions (net cash paid, "Cash Acquisitions" line) | $3,188mm | USD | 15.2% | [CIQ Financials_Annual export, Cash Flow tab, "Cash Acquisitions" row, FY2021 $(2,314)mm + FY2022 $(59)mm + FY2023 $0 + FY2024 $0 + FY2025 $(815)mm] |
| Buybacks | $7,775mm | USD | 37.0% | [CIQ Financials_Annual export, Cash Flow tab, "Repurchase of Common Stock" row, FY2024 $(1,252)mm + FY2025 $(6,523)mm; FY21–23 = $0] |
| Dividends | $0mm | USD | 0.0% | [CIQ Financials_Annual export, Cash Flow tab, "Total Dividends Paid" row = 0 in every column 2016–LTM] |
| Capex | $1,351mm | USD | 6.4% | [CIQ Financials_Annual export, Cash Flow tab, "Capital Expenditure" row, FY21–25 sum] |
| Debt repayment (gross) | $10,617mm | USD | 50.5% | [CIQ Financials_Annual export, Cash Flow tab, "Total Debt Repaid" row, FY21–25 sum] |

Note on debt repayment: the $10,617mm of gross debt repaid over FY2021–2025 was largely refinancing, not net deleveraging — Uber also issued $11,921mm of new debt over the same five years [CIQ Financials_Annual export, Cash Flow tab, "Total Debt Issued" row, FY21–25 sum]. The net debt decline over the period (strict basis: total debt minus cash and equivalents, from $7,309mm at FY2021-end to $5,197mm at FY2025-end [earnings/01_historical-financials.md, §1]) came mainly from cash accumulation as operating cash flow (CFO) grew from $(445)mm (FY2021) to $10,099mm (FY2025), not from a deliberate program to pay debt down.

Cumulative CFO used as the denominator above is arithmetically odd because FY2021 CFO was negative $(445)mm; the five-year sum ($21,018mm) is dominated by FY2023–FY2025. Read the percentages above as directional weight of each use across the period, not a precise "cash in / cash out" split for any single year.

## 2. M&A Scorecard

| Deal | Year | Price | Disclosed Rationale / Synergy | Delivered? | Opportunity Cost (divestitures forced / focus lost / options foregone) | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---|---|
| Transplace (folded into Uber Freight) | Closed Nov-2021 | ~$2.25bn, mostly cash (part-financed by $550mm of third-party minority investment into Freight Holding from ADG/D1 Capital/GCM Grosvenor) | Build a managed-transportation/logistics platform to scale Uber Freight into a full "network orchestrator" for shippers and carriers | **Not delivered.** Freight segment revenue jumped to $6,947mm in the first full post-deal year (FY2022) but then FELL three straight years — $5,245mm (FY2023, −24.5%), $5,141mm (FY2024, −2.0%), $5,099mm (FY2025, −0.8%) — and Freight EBITDA has stayed negative in every measurable year since: $(64)mm (FY2023), $(74)mm (FY2024), $(33)mm (FY2025) [CIQ Financials_Annual export, Segments tab]. Four years and $2.25bn later, the acquired business is smaller and still loses money. | Freight has consumed management attention and roughly $2.25bn of capital that could have gone to buybacks (running at $0 in FY2021–2023) or the still-declining net-debt position; no evidence in this pool of a strategic option foregone elsewhere, but the segment itself is the clearest example of capital not creating per-share value. | [CIQ Financials_Annual export, Segments tab, Freight revenue/EBITDA rows FY2021–2025]; [Web: CNBC/Dallas Innovates/FreightWaves, Jul–Nov 2021, unverified — deal price and financing structure] |
| Drizly (alcohol-delivery marketplace) | Acquired Feb-2021 (all-stock, ~$1.1bn); shut down Mar-2024 | ~$1.1bn, all-stock | Add alcohol delivery as a new vertical inside the Delivery segment | **Not delivered — reversed.** Uber shut Drizly down entirely three years after buying it, folding its marketplace into Uber Eats and stating the standalone app no longer fit its "single app" strategy. The ~$1.1bn purchase consideration was not recovered as a standalone asset. | A full write-off of a discrete, once-standalone acquisition — capital and three years of integration effort spent on a business subsequently shut rather than sold or scaled, with no disclosed residual value recovered. | [Web: CBS News / CNN / Axios / TechTimes, Jan-2024, unverified, dated — deal price and shutdown]; not present in this data pool's Key Developments export, which does not cover 2021–2024 |
| Careem Technologies stake (divestiture then partial buy-back) | e& acquired majority Careem Technologies stake from Uber for ~$400mm (Dec-2023, web-sourced); Uber then agreed to buy 12.5% of that same entity back from e& for $100mm (Jun-2026) | Net: sold majority control in 2023, is now repurchasing a slice of the same asset in 2026 | Separate Careem's non-ride-hail "super-app" businesses into Careem Technologies and rebalance ownership with e&, Uber's regional telecom partner; the 2026 step reduces e&'s stake from 50.03% to 37.53% and gives Uber a call option on the remainder (2031–2032) | **Round trip, not a value-creation event.** Selling majority control of an asset in 2023 and buying a slice of it back in 2026 (implying a ~$800mm valuation for 100% of Careem Technologies at the $100mm/12.5% price) reads as strategic drift rather than a coherent, once-decided allocation of capital, though the dollar amounts are individually small (0.07% of Uber's ~$139bn market cap for the 2026 step) [CIQ Public Company Profile, Market Cap $139,261.7mm]. | Small in dollar terms but symptomatic of the broader pattern below (frequent, opportunistic portfolio moves rather than a small number of large, high-conviction bets). | [CIQ Key Developments export, Careem Technologies / e& entry, Jun-1-2026, $100mm / 12.5% stake]; [Web: The National / Fintech News UAE / Wamda, Jun-2026, unverified — 2023 divestiture price and stake mechanics] |
| Segments.ai BV (AI/robotics data-annotation tooling) | Oct-2025 | Not disclosed | Build annotation tooling for robotics/autonomous-vehicle programs under Uber AI Solutions | Too early to assess — a small, recent tuck-in with no disclosed price or standalone financial impact | Minor — small bolt-on, not a capital-allocation swing factor on its own | [CIQ Key Developments export, "Uber Technologies, Inc. (NYSE:UBER) acquired Segments.ai BV", Oct-2-2025] |
| SpotHero, Inc. (parking marketplace) | Agreed Feb-2026, expected to close H1 2026 | Not disclosed | Not detailed in the pool beyond the deal announcement | Too early to assess — pending/recently closing, no post-close operating data in this pool | Minor on its own; adds to deal count in the serial-acquirer pattern (see below) | [CIQ Key Developments export, "Uber Technologies, Inc. have reached an agreement to acquire SpotHero, Inc.", Feb-23-2026] |
| Delivery Portfolio in Türkiye (Getir Perakende Lojistik) + 15% stake in Getir's grocery/retail/water business | Agreed Feb-2026; food-delivery leg approved by the Turkish Competition Board Jun-19-2026, expected to close H2 2026 | ~$340mm total ($335mm cash for the delivery-portfolio assets + $100mm for a 15% stake in the separate grocery/retail/water business) | Consolidate Delivery-segment position in Turkey | Too early to assess — not yet closed | Minor on its own; another market-by-market bolt-on rather than a scaled platform move | [CIQ Key Developments export, "Uber Technologies, Inc. entered into an agreement to acquire Delivery Portfolio in Türkiye...", Feb-8-2026 and Jun-19-2026 update] |
| Blacklane GmbH (premium chauffeur/black-car booking platform) | Agreed Mar-30-2026, expected to close by end of 2026 | $1.1bn | Not detailed beyond the deal announcement in this pool | Too early to assess — not yet closed | Minor on its own; a second $1bn+ deal announced within 8 months of the Delivery Hero approach, reinforcing the acquisition-pace signal | [CIQ Key Developments export, "Uber Technologies, Inc. entered into an agreement to acquire Blacklane GmbH for $1.1 billion", Mar-30-2026] |
| Delivery Hero SE — additional 63.21% stake (targeting ~100% economic interest) | Agreed Jul-16-2026; offer expected H2 2027 | €12.9bn equity value (~$14.8bn), €41.5/share cash; funded mainly via a new ~€14.2bn committed bridge facility plus a $3.0bn term loan ($2.0bn drawn) against an existing debt base of $12.6bn aggregate principal | "Expand our reach to nearly 100 markets," stated to be accretive to Non-GAAP EPS on close and by a high-single-digit percentage by year three [Q2 FY26 transcript; CIQ Key Developments export] | **Pending — the single largest, least-proven capital-allocation decision in this scorecard.** Would roughly double Uber's total debt to buy a business Uber already competes with directly through Uber Eats. | Closing REQUIRES selling the overlapping Uber Eats/Delivery Hero businesses in 14 markets to a third party (SSW Partners, $1.6bn) — a direct, quantified example of the "geese sold to buy a hen" opportunity-cost pattern (§24 Filter 4): existing, already-built market positions divested under deal pressure rather than the combination being a clean bolt-on. A €700mm/€200mm break-fee structure (Uber pays €700mm, Delivery Hero pays €200mm on termination) also puts real capital at risk before the deal even closes. | [CIQ Key Developments export, Delivery Hero Business Combination Agreement entry, Jul-16-2026]; [Q2 FY26 10-Q, "Pending Acquisition of Delivery Hero," Liquidity and Capital Resources section]; [Q2 FY26 transcript, prepared remarks] |

**Serial-acquirer + opportunity-cost test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 4) — TRIPPED.** Uber has done roughly a dozen acquisitions since its 2019 IPO and has announced or closed at least five more in the eight months leading into this report (Segments.ai, SpotHero, Getir Turkey, Blacklane, the Careem stake purchase) on top of the $14.8bn Delivery Hero deal — a clear serial-acquirer pattern, not a small number of high-conviction bets. Of the two largest deals where post-close financial results are actually observable in this data pool (Transplace/Uber Freight, Drizly), NEITHER delivered: Freight's revenue has fallen for three straight years and the segment remains loss-making four years after the $2.25bn purchase, and Drizly was shut down entirely within three years of its ~$1.1bn purchase. The Delivery Hero deal is now charged its own opportunity cost, not just its own (unproven) return: closing it requires divesting overlapping Uber Eats operations in 14 markets for $1.6bn and roughly doubling total debt via a new €14.2bn bridge facility. **RF-CAP-004 is triggered.** Per the module's Score Cap Rules, this caps the Capital-Allocation Score at 50/100 regardless of the strength of the organic-reinvestment and improving-buyback signals below, and floors Governance Risk at 60 (that composite calculation belongs to `99_management-governance-synthesis`, not this agent, but the finding and its cap are recorded here).

## 3. Buyback Scorecard

This module runs before valuation; no intrinsic/fair-value judgment is made here (valuation module has not yet run for this ticker in this analysis cycle). Judgment below rests only on observable evidence: net share-count change, buyback price versus the year's own trading range, whether buybacks ran while leverage rose, and whether they merely offset stock-based-compensation (SBC) dilution.

| Year | Amount Spent | Currency | Shares Bought | Implied Avg Price | Share-Count Change (period-end) | Offset SBC Dilution? | Price-Discipline Evidence | Judgment | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---|---|---|
| FY2021–FY2023 | $0 | USD | 0 | N/A | Shares rose every year: 1,949.3mm (FY21) → 2,005.5mm (FY22) → 2,071.1mm (FY23) | No — no buyback program was active; SBC-driven issuance ran unopposed | N/A | No buyback discipline to assess — capital went to debt repayment/refinancing and modest capex instead | [CIQ Financials_Annual export, Balance Sheet tab, "Total Shares Out. on Balance Sheet Date" row] |
| FY2024 | $1,252mm (10-K narrative rounds this to "$1.2bn," excluding broker commissions/fees) | USD | 17.8mm | ≈$70.34/share ($1,252mm ÷ 17.8mm; the 10-K's own $1.2bn/17.8mm gives ≈$67.42/share excluding fees) | 2,071.1mm (FY23) → 2,108.0mm (FY24): **+36.9mm (+1.8%), INCREASED** | **No.** SBC and option exercises added more shares than the buyback removed | FY2024 UBER traded roughly $57.58–$86.34 (Web: annual price-history aggregators, unverified, dated); $70.34 sits close to the middle of that range, not clearly a buy-the-dip price | **Weak.** The first year of the new $27bn authorization was far too small to offset dilution — net share count still rose | [FY25 10-K, "Share Repurchase Authorization"]; [CIQ Financials_Annual export, Cash Flow tab, "Repurchase of Common Stock" row]; [Web: annual UBER price-range aggregators, 2024, unverified] |

**RF-CAP-002 test (buybacks not reducing share count).** Trips for FY2021–FY2024 (four of the five years in the scorecard window, net share count rose in all of them despite an active $27bn authorization from Feb-2024) and clears only in FY2025–1H2026. This is recorded as a historical Amber finding, not an open Red flag, because the most recent evidence (FY2025, 1H2026) shows the program has started actually reducing count — but three of five years failing this test is a material share of the record.

## 4. Dividends & Organic Reinvestment

| Metric | Value | Source |
|---|---:|---|
| Payout ratio | 0% — Uber has never paid a dividend in its reported history (2016–LTM) | [CIQ Financials_Annual export, Cash Flow tab, "Total Dividends Paid" row = 0 every column] |
| Dividend coverage (FCF) | Not applicable — no dividend to cover | [as above] |
| Incremental ROIC (Δ NOPAT / Δ invested capital), FY2023→FY2025 | ≈24.6% | Computed: NOPAT ≈ EBIT × (1 − 21% statutory US federal tax rate); Invested Capital ≈ Total Debt + Total Equity − Cash & Equivalents. FY2023: EBIT $1,110mm → NOPAT ≈$877mm; IC = $11,702mm debt + $12,682mm equity − $4,208mm cash = $20,176mm. FY2025: EBIT $5,565mm → NOPAT ≈$4,396mm; IC = $12,302mm debt + $28,083mm equity − $5,893mm cash = $34,492mm. ΔNOPAT = $3,519mm; ΔIC = $14,316mm; ΔNOPAT/ΔIC ≈ 24.6% [CIQ Financials_Annual export, Balance Sheet and Income Statement tabs; earnings/01_historical-financials.md, §1] |

The FY2021→FY2025 version of this same calculation produces a distorted ≈63% figure because FY2021's EBIT was deeply negative $(3,834)mm — the swing captures the return to profitability itself, not a steady-state return on newly invested capital, so it is not used as the headline number. The FY2023–FY2025 window (both years already profitable) is the more representative read: capital committed to the organic platform since FY2023 has earned an incremental return well above a reasonable cost of capital (roughly 8–10% for a company of this risk profile — inference, not from filings), even before crediting the asset-light capex discipline noted in §1 (capex ran at just 6.4% of cumulative CFO over the period, versus depreciation & amortization of $747mm in FY2025 alone [CIQ Financials_Annual export, Cash Flow tab]). This is the strongest part of Uber's capital-allocation record and stands in sharp contrast to the M&A scorecard above.

## 5. Capital-Allocation Read

Uber's capital allocation is genuinely two different records living in the same company. The organic business is capital-light and highly capital-efficient — capex has stayed near 6–7% of cumulative operating cash flow, and the incremental return on capital committed since the business turned profitable (FY2023–FY2025) works out to roughly 24.6% [§4], comfortably above any reasonable cost of capital. Buybacks were absent for three straight years (FY2021–2023) while share count kept rising on stock-based compensation, only started reducing net share count in FY2025, and even then were priced, on average, above where the stock trades today [§3] — an improving but not yet fully proven use of cash. M&A is where the record breaks down: of the two largest deals with observable post-close results in this pool, Uber Freight's $2.25bn Transplace purchase has produced three straight years of segment revenue decline and continued losses, and the $1.1bn Drizly acquisition was shut down entirely within three years [§2]. The single biggest capital-allocation signal is the pending $14.8bn Delivery Hero acquisition, financed mainly with a new €14.2bn bridge loan that would roughly double Uber's debt, to buy a business Uber already competes with directly — and closing it requires selling overlapping operations in 14 markets for $1.6bn, a quantified opportunity cost, not a hypothetical one [§2]. Net verdict: **mixed** — capital deployed inside the existing platform (organic reinvestment, and buybacks since FY2025) has created real per-share value; capital deployed to buy other companies has, on the evidence available, mostly not, and the company's largest-ever bet on that second approach is still pending.

## management-governance / 03_incentives-and-compensation.md

_Source: `03_incentives-and-compensation.md`_

# Incentives & Compensation — UBER

**Partial-data notice.** No compensation disclosure (proxy / DEF 14A) is present in this data pool. Uber's FY2025 10-K explicitly incorporates every governance/comp item by reference to a document not in the pool: Item 11 (Executive Compensation), Item 12 (Security Ownership / Equity Compensation Plan Information), Item 13 (Related-Person Transactions), and Item 14 (Accounting Fees) are all stated as "included... in the 2026 Proxy Statement and... incorporated herein by reference" [FY25 10-K, Items 11–14, p. ITEM 11/12/13/14 section]. That Proxy Statement (a DEF 14A/DEFR14A was filed with the SEC in April 2026, per a web search) was not ingested into this pool and is therefore not available as a citable source under this module's evidence rules. Per this agent's partial-data rule, **incentive alignment cannot be assessed** from the pool. What follows extracts the only high-level structure the 10-K itself discloses (equity-plan mechanics and company-wide stock-based compensation expense — not NEO-specific pay) and uses the web strictly for peer-comparison context, labeled as such. No metric weights are fabricated or asserted for Uber.

## 1. Compensation Structure

| Executive | Base | Annual Bonus | Long-Term Incentive | Cash vs Equity Mix | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| CEO — Dara Khosrowshahi | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Item 11 incorporated by reference to the 2026 Proxy Statement (not in pool) [FY25 10-K, Item 11] |
| CFO — Balaji Krishnamurthy (in role since Feb 16, 2026) | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Item 11 incorporated by reference to the 2026 Proxy Statement (not in pool) [FY25 10-K, Item 11] |

Reporting currency would be USD; reporting period would be FY2025 (calendar year ended Dec 31, 2025) under a 2026 proxy — both stated for completeness, not as evidence of actual disclosed figures, since no Summary Compensation Table is present in this pool.

**What the 10-K does disclose (equity-plan mechanics, not individual pay):** Uber grants awards under the 2019 Equity Incentive Plan and the 2019 Employee Stock Purchase Plan (ESPP), covering "officers and other employees, directors, and consultants" — not broken out by named executive [FY25 10-K, Note 10 – Stockholders' Equity]. Three award types exist: (1) **service-based** RSUs/options vesting over a multi-year requisite service period; (2) **performance-based** awards (RSAs, RSUs, SARs, options) that vest on both service and "specified performance targets, such as our financial or operating metrics" — the specific metrics are not named in the 10-K; and (3) **market-based** awards that vest on "achievement of specified fully-diluted equity values, as determined based on our stock price," valued using a Monte Carlo model [FY25 10-K, Note 10, Stock-Based Compensation Expense section]. The 10-K states market-based awards granted in FY2023–FY2025 were "not material," implying the legacy market-based structure (economically a form of total-shareholder-return/stock-price hurdle) is a smaller, possibly legacy, component of the current program rather than its core [FY25 10-K, Note 10]. Total fair value of RSUs vested was $1.7 billion in each of FY2023, FY2024, and FY2025 (company-wide, all employees) [FY25 10-K, Note 10]. Total stock-based compensation expense by function: FY2023 $1,935mm, FY2024 $1,796mm, FY2025 $1,826mm (company-wide, all cost centers combined — not CEO/CFO-specific) [FY25 10-K, Note 10, Stock-Based Compensation Expense table].

## 2. Performance Metrics

| Plan | Metric | Weight | Per-Share/Returns or Size-Based? | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|
| Annual bonus | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Not assessable | Incorporated by reference to 2026 Proxy Statement, not in pool [FY25 10-K, Item 11] |
| Long-term incentive — performance-based tranche | "financial or operating metrics" (unnamed) | Not disclosed | Not classifiable — the 10-K names the category (performance awards) but not the metric or weight | [FY25 10-K, Note 10] |
| Long-term incentive — market-based tranche | Fully-diluted equity value / stock-price hurdle (Monte Carlo-valued) | Not disclosed; described as "not material" for grants in FY2023–FY2025 | This category, where it exists, is stock-price-based — closer to a per-share/returns signal than a size metric, but it is a small and possibly shrinking part of the program on the company's own "not material" characterization | [FY25 10-K, Note 10] |
| Long-term incentive — service-based tranche | Time vesting only (no performance condition) | Not disclosed | Not performance-linked at all — pure retention/dilution mechanism, not evidence of either per-share or size alignment | [FY25 10-K, Note 10] |

No bonus-plan metric or LTIP performance-metric weight can be extracted from this pool. The one performance-linked category the 10-K names in outline (performance-based awards tied to "financial or operating metrics") is not decomposable into ROIC/EPS/FCF-per-share versus revenue/EBITDA/deal-count without the Compensation Discussion & Analysis section of the 2026 Proxy Statement, which is absent from the pool.

## 3. Pay vs Performance

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| CEO total pay (latest) | Not disclosed in this pool. (A third-party aggregator site references CEO pay data pulled from SEC filings, but that figure is not independently verified against the primary proxy in this pool and is not used here per this agent's rule against citing a vendor number under a filing's name.) | Not assessable from pool [FY25 10-K, Item 11 — incorporated by reference, absent] |
| Pay vs peers (web-sourced context only) | Peer rideshare/delivery platform Lyft disclosed, in its own FY2025 proxy commentary, that performance stock units (PSUs) remain roughly 50% of each named executive's target annual equity value, with an increasing emphasis on performance-linked equity — offered here only as sector-context on what "typical" gig-platform LTIP design currently looks like, not as a statement about Uber's own program. | Web: Lyft DEFC14A / DEF 14A proxy coverage, 2025 (unverified, dated) — peer-benchmarking context only, per this agent's partial-data rule |
| Pay-for-performance alignment | Not assessable — no bonus/LTIP metric, weight, or target is available in the pool to test against realized performance (e.g., the four straight quarters of Adjusted EBITDA guidance beats documented in `01_management-and-track-record.md`). | Not assessable |
| Say-on-pay support (%) | Not disclosed in this pool — say-on-pay vote results are reported in the proxy/AGM voting-results filing, which is not present. | Not assessable [FY25 10-K, Item 11 — incorporated by reference, absent] |

## 4. Incentive Alignment Read

Incentive alignment cannot be assessed from the data available in this pool: the 10-K's Items 11–14 all point to the 2026 Proxy Statement for the actual pay tables, bonus/LTIP metrics, weights, targets, ownership, and say-on-pay results, and that document was not ingested. The only structural facts the 10-K itself discloses are that Uber's long-term equity program mixes pure time-vesting awards with performance-based awards tied to unnamed "financial or operating metrics" and a smaller, currently "not material" stock-price-hurdle (market-based) tranche — which is not enough to classify the program as per-share/returns-based or size-based with any confidence. The single biggest incentive signal available is the absence of the primary evidence itself: without the Compensation Discussion & Analysis, this agent cannot say whether Khosrowshahi and Krishnamurthy are paid on revenue/deal growth (empire-building) or on ROIC/EPS/FCF-per-share/TSR (per-share value) — that is the single highest-value follow-up data request for this module. **Conclusion: Insufficient data — not assessable** (neither "well-aligned," "mixed," nor "misaligned" can be supported by evidence in this pool; do not read the absence of red flags here as a clean bill of health).

## management-governance / 04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md

_Source: `04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md`_

# Ownership & Insider Behavior — UBER

## Partial-Data Notice

The 2026 Proxy Statement (DEF 14A) — the primary source for a beneficial-ownership percentage table, Section 16(a) delinquency disclosures, and Schedule 13D/13G filings — is **not in the data pool**; the FY25 10-K's Item 12 explicitly defers to it: "The information required by this item is included under the headers 'Stock Ownership Information...' and 'Equity Compensation Plan Information' in the 2026 Proxy Statement and is incorporated herein by reference" [FY25 10-K, Item 12, p.33163]. This is a genuine data gap for a US filer, already logged in `00_governance-data-triage.md`.

What partly fills the gap: `UberTechnologiesIncNYSEUBER_CIQReportLandscape.txt` (Capital IQ) carries its own **Public Ownership Summary**, **Top 25 Holders**, and **Insider Trading Activity Summary / Recent Insider Purchases / Recent Insider Sales** tables — a vendor aggregation of the same underlying Section 16 (Form 3/4) and institutional 13F/13D/13G filings, sitting in this data pool (not web-sourced, not memory). Per this module's source hierarchy this is tier 4 ("Capital IQ / Bloomberg — ownership, insider transactions"), one rank below the proxy/DEF 14A itself. All ownership and insider-transaction figures below are cited to this pool document; confidence is capped at 3/5 (vendor-aggregated, not the primary SEC filing) rather than the 5/5 a DEF 14A or raw Form 4 would carry. No claim in this report is web- or memory-sourced.

## 1. Ownership

| Holder | % Held | Share Class | Bought or Granted? | Source |
|---|---:|---|---|---|
| CEO — Dara Khosrowshahi | 0.060% (1,225,802 shs, $87.8mm) | Common Stock (single class) | Granted (equity comp; see §2 — recurring vest/sell pattern, no open-market cash buy identified) | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Top 25 Holders (Individuals/Insiders)"] |
| Other insiders (aggregate — officers & directors) | 0.176% (3,586,107 shs, $256.8mm) per the Top-25 table; 0.18% ($244.5mm) per the Public Ownership Summary snapshot — the two CIQ tables differ slightly on timing/rounding, both cited | Common Stock | Almost entirely granted (RSU vesting); one CFO option exercise identified (see §2) | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Public Ownership Summary" and "Top 25 Holders (Individuals/Insiders)"] |
| Largest institutional holder — BlackRock, Inc. | 7.417% (150,987,293 shs, $10,812.2mm) | Common Stock | N/A (asset manager, passive/index-heavy) | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Top 25 Holders (Institutions)"] |
| Promoter / controlling bloc | **None.** No promoter or controlling-shareholder group exists; Institutions hold 84.00% of shares outstanding across dozens of managers, no single holder above 7.5% | Common Stock | N/A | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Public Ownership Summary"; FY25 10-K, Item 5, p.5289 — 1,207 holders of record] |

**Total insider (officer + director) ownership: ~0.18% of shares outstanding** (2,042,560,121 total common-stock-equivalent shares outstanding) [CIQ Landscape Report, "Public Ownership Summary"]. This is a widely-held, professionally-managed company — both co-founders are gone from executive and voting-board roles, and Travis Kalanick fully divested his stake by 2023 [business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md, "Insider / promoter ownership" row; management-governance/01_management-and-track-record.md, §"Founder / owner-operator status"]. Co-founder Garrett Camp (Board Observer & Product Advisor, non-voting) does not appear in the CIQ Top-25 Individuals/Insiders table — not proven from available data whether he still holds a reportable UBER common-stock position; flagged as a follow-up.

Other notable non-insider holders with board or strategic relevance: **Public Investment Fund** (Saudi Arabia's sovereign wealth fund) holds 3.578% ($5,216.1mm) and has one board seat — Independent Director Turqi Abdulrahman-A. Alnowaiser is PIF's own Deputy Governor & Head of International Investment [CIQ Landscape Report, "Top 25 Holders (Institutions)"; CIQ Board Members export, Alnowaiser bio]. **Pershing Square Capital Management, L.P.** (Bill Ackman) holds 1.472% ($2,145.3mm) [CIQ Landscape Report, "Top 25 Holders (Institutions)"] — a concentrated, activist-style investor position, distinct from the index/passive majority of the register.

|---|---|---:|---|---|---|

| Dara Khosrowshahi (CEO) | Buy (vest) | 243,258 shs, $0 value | Mar-16-2026 | $0 value = RSU vesting/settlement, not a cash purchase | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Recent Insider Purchases (Last 6 Months)"] |
| West, Ceremony, Krishnamurthy (CFO), Hazelbaker, MacDonald | Buy (vest) | Small recurring lots (~1,800–4,700 shs each), $0 value | Recurring on the 16th of each month, Feb–Jul 2026 | $0 value = scheduled RSU vesting, not cash purchases; the fixed monthly date across five different executives is consistent with a standing vest/settle schedule (10b5-1 status not stated in pool) | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Recent Insider Purchases (Last 6 Months)"] |
| Dara Khosrowshahi (CEO) | Sell | (253,700) shs, $18,941,242 @ $74.66 | Mar-16-2026 | Same-day as his vesting event above; sold more shares than vested that day, so a net reduction, not pure sell-to-cover; 10b5-1 status not stated in pool | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Recent Insider Sales (Last 6 Months)"] |
| West, Ceremony, Krishnamurthy Nikki (former CPO), Hazelbaker, MacDonald | Sell | ~$4.1mm–$6.5mm each @ $74.66 | Mar-16-2026 | Same-day multi-executive sale cluster, coinciding with the larger vesting tranche that month | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Recent Insider Sales (Last 6 Months)"] |
| West, Ceremony, Krishnamurthy (CFO), Hazelbaker, MacDonald | Sell | Small recurring lots ($42K–$427K each) | Recurring on the 16th of each month, Feb–Jul 2026 | Matches the recurring vesting dates — consistent with routine sell-to-cover for tax withholding, not opportunistic timing; 10b5-1 status not stated in pool | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Recent Insider Sales (Last 6 Months)"] |

**Aggregate (per CIQ "Insider Trading Activity Summary"):**
- Last 3 months: 16 purchase transactions (51,482 shares) vs. 15 sale transactions (55,091 shares) → **net sales of 3,609 shares**.
- Last 6 months: 44 purchase transactions (574,497 shares) vs. 39 sale transactions (608,470 shares) → **net sales of 33,973 shares**.

[CIQ Landscape Report, "Insider Trading Activity Summary"]

**Net insider activity: net selling**, both over 3 and 6 months, but modest relative to the 3.59mm shares insiders hold in aggregate (roughly 1% of insider-held shares sold net over six months). Nearly all "purchases" in the pool data are $0-value RSU vesting events, not open-market cash buys — the one clear exception (CFO Krishnamurthy's $1.6mm Feb-24-2026 transaction) reads as an option exercise, itself typically paired with an immediate offsetting sale rather than a discretionary conviction buy. **No open-market, discretionary cash purchase by any officer or director is identifiable in this pool's trailing-six-month data** — this is compensation cycling through vest-and-sell, not insiders putting new personal cash into the stock.

## 3. Pledging & Encumbrance

| Insider | Shares Pledged | % of Their Holding | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---|

**No pledging disclosed.** Uber's Insider Trading Policy contractually prohibits it: "You are prohibited from hedging, pledging and lending Company securities in any transaction..." [FY25 10-K, Exhibit — Insider Trading Policy, p.36240–36245]. `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` confirms this is answered directly by the policy prohibition rather than a filed pledge disclosure, since Uber has no promoter/controlling-shareholder group requiring one [business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md, disqualifier #2 row]. Separately and not to be confused with insider share pledging: Uber has pledged its own **corporate investment** in Aurora Innovation Class A common stock (an equity stake Uber holds as an investment) as collateral for its 2028 Exchangeable Senior Notes [FY25 10-K, p.18289; Note 8 — Long-Term Debt and Credit Arrangements]. That is a corporate balance-sheet financing matter, not insider personal-holding pledging, and belongs to the balance-sheet-survival module, not this one — flagged here only to avoid conflation.

## 4. Control Structure

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Dual-class / super-voting | **None.** Uber has a single registered class: "Common Stock, par value $0.00001 per share," one vote per share | [FY25 10-K, cover page, p.221 region] |
| Controlled-company status | Not a controlled company. No promoter/founder/family bloc; largest single holder (BlackRock) holds 7.417% | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Top 25 Holders (Institutions)"] |
| Shareholder bloc with board rights | Public Investment Fund (PIF, Saudi sovereign wealth fund) holds 3.578% ($5.2bn) and has one board seat via its own Deputy Governor, Turqi Alnowaiser, an Independent Director since Nov-2023 — a large, semi-strategic minority holder, well short of control | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Top 25 Holders (Institutions)"; CIQ Board Members export, Alnowaiser bio] |
| Controlling-owner objective alignment *(Filter 6)* | **Not applicable — no controlling owner exists.** Ownership is dispersed across institutions (84.00%), the public float (15.34%), and a residual insider stake (0.18%); the single largest holder (BlackRock, 7.4%) is a diversified asset manager, not an operating parent, a government, or a conglomerate steering capital elsewhere | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Public Ownership Summary"] |

**Unaligned-owner test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 6).** None of the three disqualifying structures applies. Uber is not government-controlled — PIF's 3.578% stake plus one of eleven board seats is a minority position with representation, not control, and the remaining 84% institutional float is spread across dozens of independent managers with no coordinated bloc. Uber is not a listed subsidiary of a value-maximizing parent — it has no parent. Uber is not itself a sprawling unrelated-diversified conglomerate at the ownership level (its M&A pattern — a serial-acquirer concern, including the pending Delivery Hero takeover — is a capital-allocation issue owned by `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` and this module's `02_capital-allocation-scorecard`, not an ownership-control conflict with minority holders). **RF-OWN-004 is not triggered.**

## 4A. Market Conduct

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Insider buys/sells vs subsequent results (timing) | The recurring monthly sales fall on the 16th of each month across five different executives — a pattern consistent with a standing vesting/settlement schedule, not opportunistic timing. The one large multi-executive sale cluster (Mar-16-2026, ~$38mm combined across Khosrowshahi, West, Hazelbaker, Krishnamurthy Nikki, MacDonald) predates the next earnings release (May-06-2026) by seven weeks and predates the Delivery Hero deal disclosures (May–Jul 2026) by two-plus months; no filing or disclosure in the pool ties it to non-public adverse information. Not proven from available data that this was timed ahead of bad news — but the pool lacks the raw Form 4 filing dates/codes needed for a rigorous event-study, so this read carries only moderate confidence | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Recent Insider Sales (Last 6 Months)"; Events Calendar export] |
| Unusual price / volume before announcements | **Not assessable from this pool** — no daily price/volume time series is available beyond the single delayed quote snapshot and the 52-week high/low ($101.99 / $65.41) | [Public Company Profile export] |
| Exchange clarification sought for a price move | Not applicable — no such disclosure mechanism triggered/found for a US NYSE listing in this pool | — |
| Institutional (top-holder) trend; any sharp high-quality-holder exit | Institutions hold 84.00% of shares outstanding, led by BlackRock (7.417%), Capital Research (6.670%), Vanguard entities (6.569% + 1.872% + 0.583%), State Street (4.453%), and PIF (3.578%). Two flagged data points: (1) **Toyota Motor Corporation fully exited its public common-stock position** — down (5,125,868) shares to zero per the "Top Sellers" table, a full sale by a strategic corporate investor, though small (~0.25% of shares); (2) "The Vanguard Group, Inc." shows a (192,499,602)-share reduction to zero while three other Vanguard-family entities (Vanguard Capital Management, Vanguard Portfolio Management, Vanguard Fiduciary Trust) show new positions summing to ~183.7mm shares — this reads as an internal Vanguard reporting-entity reclassification, not a real institutional exit, and is flagged as a data-interpretation caveat rather than a sell signal. No evidence of a broad, high-quality institutional exodus | [CIQ Landscape Report, "Public Ownership Summary," "Top 25 Holders (Institutions)," "Top Buyers," "Top Sellers"] |

No insider selling-before-weak-results or unusual price/volume pattern is established on the evidence in this pool; both RF-MKT-001 and RF-MKT-002 are **not triggered** for lack of the granular Form 4 timing/code data and daily price series that would be needed to prove either.

## 5. Ownership / Insider Read

Insiders own a rounding error of Uber — about 0.18% of shares outstanding, and the CEO's own $87.8 million stake is just 0.06% of the company. Nearly every insider "purchase" in the trailing six months is a $0-value RSU vesting event rather than a cash buy, so this is compensation cycling through the register, not conviction; no officer or director made an identifiable open-market cash purchase in the period, and insiders were modest net sellers (down 33,973 shares over six months) in a pattern that looks scheduled rather than opportunistic. The biggest risk to minority holders here is not control or pledging — Uber has one class of stock, no promoter, and a contractual pledging ban — it is that the people running the company have almost no personal capital riding on the per-share outcome, which puts the weight of alignment entirely on incentive design (module 03) rather than on ownership (this module).
