## management-governance / 05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md

_Source: `05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md`_

# Board & Shareholder Rights — UBER

## 1. Board Composition

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Board size | 10 voting directors (all elected at the May 4, 2026 annual meeting), plus 1 non-voting Board Observer (co-founder Garrett Camp, no vote since May 2020) | [CIQ Board Members export, full roster]; [Web: StockTitan summary of Uber 8-K, "Reports Material Event," 2026-05-04 meeting — 10 nominees elected, unverified aggregation of SEC filing] |
| Independent directors (%) | 9 of 10 voting directors are independent (90%) — only CEO Dara Khosrowshahi is a management director. Independent: Ronald D. Sugar (Chair), Robert A. Eckert, John A. Thain, Revathi Advaithi, Turqi Alnowaiser, Nikesh Arora, Ursula Burns, Amanda Ginsberg, Alexander Wynaendts | [CIQ Board Members export, individual entries: "Independent Director" / "Independent Chairman" role tags] |
| Chair / CEO split? | Yes. Ronald D. Sugar has been independent, non-executive Chairman since August 2018; Khosrowshahi has been CEO (and a director, not Chairman) since September 2017. Clean separation of the two roles. | [CIQ Board Members export, Sugar entry: "Independent Chairman... since August 2018"] |
| Audit / Comp / Nominating committee independence | Audit Committee: Thain (Chair), Advaithi, Alnowaiser, Burns, Wynaendts — 5 members, 100% independent. Compensation Committee: Eckert (Chair), Sugar, Arora, Ginsberg — 4 members, 100% independent. Nominating & Corporate Governance Committee (CIQ lists it under both names with overlapping membership): Sugar (Chair of both), Eckert, Arora, Ginsberg, Burns — 100% independent. No management director (Khosrowshahi) sits on any of the three committees. | [CIQ Board Members export, "Job Functions" field per director] |

**Read.** On paper this is a well-formed governance box: a 90%-independent board, a split chair/CEO structure, and fully independent audit, compensation, and nominating/governance committees. That structural cleanliness is real evidence, not decoration — but it answers "is the board legally independent," not "does it actually check management" (Section 5 addresses the latter).

## 2. Tenure & Refreshment

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Average director tenure | ~5.9 years among the 9 independent directors (Sugar ~8.0, Eckert ~6.2, Thain ~9.0, Advaithi ~6.1, Alnowaiser ~2.8, Arora ~1.2, Burns ~9.0, Ginsberg ~6.0, Wynaendts ~5.4 years as of Aug 2026), calculated from each director's stated "Years on Board" start date to 2026-08-08. Not a stale board — a mix of long-serving (Thain, Burns since 2017) and recently added (Arora since Jun 2025, Alnowaiser since Nov 2023) members. | [CIQ Board Members export, "Years on Board" field per director; calculation as of 2026-08-08] |
| Overboarding / staleness signs | Two directors carry a heavier external-board load than is typical. (1) Turqi Alnowaiser — Deputy Governor & Head of International Investment at Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund (a full-time executive role at a sovereign wealth fund managing hundreds of billions of dollars) — simultaneously sits as Independent Chairman of Lucid Group, and as a director/supervisory-board member of Hapag-Lloyd, Heathrow Airport Holdings, FGP Topco, and LHR Airports (the latter three are related Heathrow-group entities but are formally separate board seats), on top of Uber. (2) Nikesh Arora — sitting Chairman & CEO of Palo Alto Networks (a full-time executive role) — also sits on Compagnie Financière Richemont's board in addition to Uber's. Glass Lewis's overboarding policy for executives serving on two or more outside public boards flagged Arora specifically; this is reflected in a market-visible outcome (Section 4A). | [CIQ Board Members export, Alnowaiser and Arora entries, "Other Boards" fields]; [Web: aggregated search summary citing Glass Lewis overboarding policy applied to Arora, 2026 proxy season commentary — unverified, dated 2026] |
| Recent refreshment | Two independent directors added in the last ~3 years: Alnowaiser (Nov 2023) and Arora (Jun 2025) — both outside hires, not internal promotions. No independent-director resignations "citing concerns" appear in the pool or in web-sourced coverage. | [CIQ Board Members export, "Years on Board" fields] |

**Read.** Tenure is reasonably fresh — this is not an entrenched, decade-plus-unchanged board. The overboarding on two directors (one of whom, Arora, drew a specific proxy-advisor flag and a materially elevated votes-against count) is the one genuine staleness/bandwidth concern, not tenure length.

## 3. Related-Party Transactions & Group Leakage

| RPT Type | Amount | % of Revenue | % of PAT | % of Net Worth | Arm's Length? | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| Sales to / purchases from related parties | Not disclosed as a separate line — no evidence of related-party sales/purchases at scale | — | — | — | N/A | [FY25 10-K, Notes — no such line disclosed] |
| Loans / advances / guarantees to related parties | Term loan to Moove Cars Mobility, S.L. ("Moove"): $384mm (Dec 31, 2025) vs $288mm (Dec 31, 2024) — a loan receivable (asset), part of a 2021 equity-investment + term-loan + commercial-partnership package. Related-party liabilities to Moove disclosed as "not material." | 0.74% (of FY25 revenue $52,017mm) | 3.82% (of FY25 net income $10,053mm) | 1.42% (of FY25 stockholders' equity $27,041mm) | Equity/loan investment in an operating partner in which Uber has significant influence, not a promoter-linked deal | [FY25 10-K, Note "Investments," p.31500–31534] |
| Royalty / brand / management fees to promoter group | None — Uber has no promoter/controlling group | — | — | — | N/A | [business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md, row 2] |
| Rent / other payments to promoter entities | None disclosed | — | — | — | N/A | [FY25 10-K] |
| Outstanding receivables from related parties | Lime Convertible Note (notes receivable from Neutron Holdings, Inc. / "Lime"): $144mm (Dec 31, 2025) vs $119mm (Dec 31, 2024). Careem Technologies: equity-method minority investment (~42% retained after the Dec 2023 e& transaction, plus a further $100mm purchase of a 12.5% stake from e& in Jun 2026), initial carrying value $300mm, basis difference "not material." | 0.28% (Lime, of FY25 revenue) | 1.43% (Lime, of FY25 net income) | 0.53% (Lime, of FY25 equity) | Both are strategic minority investments with board representation (2 Careem Technologies board seats), not sales/purchases with an insider | [FY25 10-K, p.18296 (Lime); p.20538 (Careem Technologies)] |

Combined related-party receivables (Lime note $144mm + Moove term loan $384mm) = $528mm, or **1.01% of FY25 revenue**, **5.25% of FY25 net income**, and **1.95% of FY25 stockholders' equity** — all well inside the Low/Medium band on the revenue and net-worth bases (materiality thresholds are keyed to revenue/assets, not PAT; the PAT ratio is a secondary check only). These are recurring, disclosed, minority strategic-investment balances, not sales/purchase transactions with a promoter or insider group — because **Uber has no promoter or controlling shareholder** [business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md, row 2/3: "Uber has no promoter/controlling shareholder group (widely held, 1,207 holders of record as of Feb 10, 2026)"]. The 10-K does not disclose the specific board/committee approval mechanism for these related-party arrangements in this data pool (the proxy statement's Related Person Transactions Policy is incorporated by reference and is not itself in the pool); NYSE listing standards require independent-committee review of related-party transactions as a baseline requirement — **Inference, not from filings**, not independently confirmed here.

**Cross-reference:** `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` computed the same underlying Moove figure at 0.7% of revenue and confirmed no related-party transaction approaches the 25% hard-disqualifier threshold. No RF-RPT-001/002/003 is triggered — value is not leaking to a promoter or insider group because there is no such group to leak to.

## 4. Takeover Defenses & Voting Rights

| Feature | Present? | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Poison pill | No formal rights plan in force, but the board can act like one at will | No active shareholder-rights plan is disclosed. However, the certificate of incorporation authorizes the board to issue **undesignated ("blank check") preferred stock without stockholder approval** — the 10-K itself states this "makes it possible for our board of directors to issue preferred stock with voting or other rights or preferences that could impede the success of any attempt to acquire us." This is the standard mechanism a board uses to adopt a pill on short notice. | [FY25 10-K, Item 1A risk factor, "Delaware law and provisions... could make a merger, tender offer, or proxy contest difficult," p. ~4799–4844] |
| Classified / staggered board | No | All 10 directors stand for election annually (confirmed by the May 4, 2026 AGM, where all 10 nominees were up for a one-year term); the 10-K's anti-takeover risk-factor list does not include a classified board among its provisions. | [FY25 10-K, Item 1A risk factor list]; [Web: search-aggregated summary of Uber's Amended & Restated Certificate of Incorporation, corroborating annual elections — unverified, dated 2026-08-08, not directly rendered] |
| Dual-class / unequal voting | No | Uber has a single class of common stock, one vote per share. (The "Class A common stock" references in the 10-K relate to Uber's minority equity stake in Aurora Innovation, a separate company — not to Uber's own capital structure.) No dual-class or founder super-voting shares exist; Uber's founders (Kalanick, Camp) hold no special voting class. | [FY25 10-K, Note "Investments" — Aurora Class A references are about the investee, not Uber's own stock]; [Web: search-aggregated summary confirming single-class voting requiring majority of outstanding shares "voting together as a single class," dated 2026-08-08, not directly rendered] |
| Majority vs plurality director voting | Majority voting, with board-rejectable resignation | Directors are elected by majority of votes cast in an uncontested election (votes "for" must exceed votes "against"); an incumbent who fails to get majority support must tender a resignation, but the **board retains discretion to reject that resignation** — a real but not automatic accountability mechanism. This is the design flagged as the reason Ronald Sugar's 8.1%-against and Nikesh Arora's 20.3%-against results (Section 4A) did NOT cost either director their seat despite one crossing the conventional "high opposition" line. | [Web: search-aggregated summary of Uber's bylaws majority-voting standard and board-rejectable-resignation policy, dated 2026-08-08, not directly rendered — corroborated by the fact that both directors were confirmed re-elected at the May 2026 AGM despite the vote counts in Section 4A] |
| Shareholder ability to call meetings / act by written consent | Both restricted | The 10-K itself discloses, as an anti-takeover risk factor, "limitations on stockholder ability to convene special stockholder meetings" and a "prohibition on cumulative voting in the election of directors." Separately, the certificate of incorporation is reported to prohibit stockholder action by written consent entirely — stockholders may only act at an annual or special meeting called per the bylaws. The board (not stockholders) also fills board vacancies created by expansion, resignation, or removal, and advance-notice bylaws constrain stockholder proposals. | [FY25 10-K, Item 1A risk factor list, p.4804–4844 — filing-sourced for the special-meeting limitation, cumulative-voting prohibition, board-fills-vacancies, and advance-notice provisions]; [Web: search-aggregated summary of the certificate of incorporation's written-consent prohibition, dated 2026-08-08, not directly rendered] |

**Read.** No active poison pill and no dual-class stock or classified board are genuine positives — Uber does not run the "founder-entrenchment" playbook seen at some 2019-era tech IPOs. But the actual bylaw/charter mechanics that ARE in place — no special-meeting right, no written consent, no cumulative voting, board-controlled vacancy filling, blank-check preferred authority, and board-rejectable director resignations — collectively hand the incumbent board and management wide latitude to resist an unwanted challenge and to insulate a director who loses majority support. This is a standard Delaware-IPO-era package, not an outlier, but it is not shareholder-friendly by construction.

## 4A. Shareholder Voting & Dilution

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| AGM votes against (remuneration / RPT / director / auditor resolutions) | May 4, 2026 AGM (1,686,358,501 shares represented, ~83% of voting power as of the March 12, 2026 record date). Say-on-pay (2025 NEO compensation): 1,416,648,050 for / 94,297,858 against / 2,985,807 abstain → **6.24% against** (healthy support). Auditor (PwC) ratification: 1,656,371,651 for / 26,917,608 against → **1.60% against** (healthy support). Say-on-pay frequency vote: annual frequency preferred by 1,495,473,260 votes vs 15,507,154 for triennial. Director elections (votes against, of for+against): **Nikesh Arora 20.31% against** (1,205,210,055 for / 307,126,841 against) — the highest opposition on the ballot; **Ronald Sugar (Chairman) 8.09% against** (1,376,324,629 for / 121,066,128 against) — second-highest; all other 8 directors ranged 0.54%–2.61% against. All 10 nominees were elected (majority-voting threshold cleared by all). | [Web: search-aggregated summary of Uber's May 2026 AGM 8-K vote-count disclosure (StockTitan / SEC-sourced), dated 2026-08-08 — specific vote tallies not independently re-rendered from the primary 8-K in this exercise; treated as Level-2 web evidence, not filing-grade] |
| Proxy-advisor recommendations (ISS / Glass Lewis / IiAS) | Reporting indicates at least one proxy advisor recommended against Nikesh Arora's election, consistent with Glass Lewis's stated overboarding policy for an executive (Arora is Chairman & CEO of Palo Alto Networks) sitting on two or more outside public-company boards (Uber and Compagnie Financière Richemont). The specific ISS/Glass Lewis report text is not accessible in this exercise. | [Web: search-aggregated summary citing a proxy-voting record showing an "Against" recommendation for Arora at the 2026 meeting, and general Glass Lewis overboarding policy language, dated 2026-08-08 — unverified, Level 2] |
| Institutional opposition to any resolution | The Arora vote (20.31% against) is the one resolution where opposition crossed the level generally treated as market-significant investor pushback; the Sugar vote (8.09% against) is elevated relative to every other director (next-highest is Khosrowshahi's 2.61%) but below that threshold. | [Same AGM vote-count source as above] |
| Dilution history (preferential allotment, warrants, QIP, ESOP pool) | Not assessed in this data pool — share-count/dilution mechanics (RSU issuance, buybacks, share count trend) are the domain of `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` and are not re-derived here. | Not applicable to this agent's scope |

**Materiality read.** RF-SHR-001 ("High votes against a key resolution") is **triggered for the Nikesh Arora director election** — 20.31% against clears the >20% threshold this module treats as a red-flag trigger for votes-against, and it is corroborated by an independent, specific cause (proxy-advisor overboarding concern), not noise. The Ronald Sugar vote (8.09% against, ~3x the median director's opposition) does not cross that threshold but is Amber — worth watching, especially given his simultaneous roles as Chairman, Compensation Committee member, and Nominating/Governance Committee chair, i.e., he sits at the center of exactly the accountability machinery being asked to police itself.

## 5. Minority-Shareholder Protection Read

The board is structurally independent and not entrenched by ownership: 90% independent directors, a split chair/CEO, fully independent audit/comp/nominating committees, no controlling promoter or dual-class stock, and related-party balances that are small (≤2% of net worth) and unconnected to any insider group — this is a genuinely different starting point from a promoter-controlled or family-controlled company, and AGM support for pay (93.8% for) and the auditor (98.4% for) confirms shareholders are not in open revolt. Where the protection weakens is procedural: shareholders cannot call a special meeting, cannot act by written consent, cannot vote cumulatively, and cannot fill their own board vacancies — the board holds every lever an unhappy shareholder base would need to force change outside the annual meeting, and a director who loses majority support (as nearly happened to no one here, but structurally could) can have his resignation rejected by the same board he sits on. The single biggest concrete risk to minority holders right now is the July 23, 2026 shareholder derivative lawsuit (Louisiana Sheriffs' Pension & Relief Fund and two other pension funds, filed in the U.S. District Court for the Northern District of California) naming CEO Khosrowshahi, Chairman Sugar, all eight other sitting directors, the company president, and two former CFOs, alleging the board ignored internal tools — including a 2017-built machine-learning risk model not deployed until 2022 — while the company's disclosed sexual-assault incident count (12,522 for 2017–2022) diverged sharply from an alleged internal total of 400,181 reports over the same period; this is an unresolved, unadjudicated allegation, not a proven finding, but it directly tests whether this board's independence on paper translated into real oversight, and it is the evidence-based reason the verdict here is not "strong." **Overall verdict: adequate** — real independence and no ownership-based entrenchment, offset by shareholder-unfriendly meeting/consent mechanics and a live, board-wide fiduciary-duty allegation that has not yet been tested.

## management-governance / 06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md

_Source: `06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`_

# Candor & Disclosure Quality — UBER

Two verbatim earnings-call transcripts are in the data pool (Q1 FY2026, May-06-2026; Q2 FY2026, Aug-05-2026) [`Uber Technologies, Inc., Q1 2026 Earnings Call, May-06-2026`; `Uber Technologies, Inc., Q2 2026 Earnings Call, Aug-05-2026`]. No shareholder letters exist for this filer (Uber does not publish one) — the module rule requires "transcripts OR prior letters," and transcripts alone satisfy it, so no partial-data cap applies to this agent [`00_governance-data-triage.md`, §5]. Both transcripts are read here as primary evidence for tone and Q&A directness. `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` is available and used for the non-GAAP cross-check. Both transcripts fall inside a beat streak (Adjusted EBITDA cleared above the guided high end in both quarters) — the pool contains no quarter in which Uber missed its own guided metric, so the sharpest test of "candor in genuinely bad times" (a guided miss) cannot be run from this data; that limitation is stated wherever it matters below rather than papered over.

## 1. Owning the Misses

| Miss (from 01) | How It Was Communicated | Owned or Obscured? | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Revenue vs. Street consensus, Q1 FY26 (−0.45%) and Q2 FY26 (−0.52%) — Uber issues no formal revenue guidance, so this is a consensus miss, not a broken promise | The CFO proactively and specifically quantified the mechanical driver when asked about take rates: "we have talked about a business model change in the U.K., which is primarily an impact on our mobility business... you are seeing a nearly 500 basis points decline year-on-year. Of that 500 basis points, about 400 basis points is entirely related to this U.K. business model change, and it's an optical impact. It moves cost from cost of revenue." | **Owned** — a specific, quantified, unprompted-in-substance explanation with an exact basis-point attribution, not a vague "headwind" | [Q2 FY26 transcript, Q&A — Balaji Krishnamurthy, CFO] |
| GAAP diluted EPS swings of −82% to +351% quarter to quarter (Q3 FY25–Q2 FY26), driven by mark-to-market moves on minority equity stakes (Aurora, Didi, Grab) | Neither transcript volunteers an explanation of GAAP EPS volatility; management's prepared remarks and Q&A consistently redirect to non-GAAP EPS ("Non-GAAP EPS increased 44% year-over-year" — Q1 FY26; "non-GAAP EPS growing 35% year-over-year" — Q2 FY26) without addressing the GAAP number's swings at all | **Partially obscured by omission** — the volatility is disclosed in the 10-K's own reconciliation table (so it is not hidden from a reader of the filing), but it is never surfaced or explained on either call, even though GAAP EPS is the headline number a casual reader sees first | [Q1 FY26 transcript, prepared remarks]; [Q2 FY26 transcript, prepared remarks]; [`earnings/06_earnings-quality.md`, §7] |
| Two federal-jury verdicts in the nationwide sexual-assault/harassment MDL (3,000+ cases): Arizona, Feb-06-2026, $8.5M in damages; Charlotte NC, Apr-20-2026, $5,000 in damages — both landing before the three most recent SEC filings | Not raised or asked about on either earnings call. In the filings, Item 3 / Note 14 (FY25 10-K) and Item 1 / Note 11 (Q1 and Q2 FY26 10-Qs) each name only two "material legal proceedings" by title — "Driver Classification" and "State Unemployment Taxes" — and fold the MDL into a generic "personal injury claims" bucket alongside an aggregate $1.8–2.1bn reserve for all legal, regulatory, and non-income-tax matters combined. Neither the FY25 10-K (filed one week after the Arizona verdict) nor either FY26 10-Q names the MDL, "bellwether," the presiding judge, or either verdict | **Obscured by omission** — not fabricated or denied, but a materially newsworthy, ongoing MDL with two adverse jury outcomes is left out of the named-matters list across three consecutive filings while smaller, older matters are named specifically | [FY25 10-K, Item 3 / Note 14]; [Q1 FY26 10-Q, Item 1 / Note 11]; [Q2 FY26 10-Q, Item 1 / Note 11]; [CIQ Key Developments, "Jury Finds Uber... Liable in Sexual Assault Case," Feb-06-2026; "Federal Jury in Charlotte... Delivers... Verdict," Apr-20-2026] |
| CFO transition (Feb-2026) and Chief People Officer departure (May-2026) landing three months apart | Company stated plainly the CFO change "is not the result of any disagreements concerning financial disclosures or accounting matters" — a direct, checkable claim, not a vague reassurance | **Owned** — specific denial of the most damaging alternative explanation, and nothing in the subsequent record contradicts it | [CIQ Key Developments, CFO transition entry, Feb-2026]; [`01_management-and-track-record.md`, §2, §4] |

## 2. Non-GAAP / Adjustment Aggressiveness

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Recurring "one-off" addbacks | The "legal, non-income tax, and regulatory reserve changes and settlements" line in the Adjusted EBITDA reconciliation was $1,123M in FY2024 and $564M in FY2025 — present, and material (6.5–17% of the total add-back), in both years the filing discloses. A charge that recurs at >$500M for two straight years is not a one-off by its own repetition | `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md`, §4–5 |
| GAAP-to-adjusted gap (magnitude / trend) | FY2025 Adjusted EBITDA ($8,730M) sat $2,446M (+38.9%) above the reported/GAAP-basis EBITDA figure ($6,284M), and $3,165M (+56.9%) above GAAP Income from operations ($5,565M). Stock-based compensation ($1,826M, 20.9% of Adjusted EBITDA) is the single largest add-back and is excluded from "adjusted" profitability every year despite being real, recurring, and dilutive | `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md`, §4, §7 |
| Does adjusted framing flatter reality? | Yes, on two counts. First, the standard non-GAAP quality issue: SBC and the recurring "legal/regulatory" charge are both excluded from the headline Adjusted EBITDA every year, making the "clean" number look better than the underlying cost structure. Second, and more serious: beginning Q1 FY2026 Uber discontinued the consolidated Adjusted EBITDA reconciliation from its 10-Q disclosures entirely (see §3 below) — so the very metric flattered by these add-backs can no longer be checked against GAAP from the primary filing at all, even as management (via the guidance and consensus process) keeps reporting quantified beats against an "EBITDA" figure of undisclosed current-year composition | `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md`, §7; `earnings/01_historical-financials.md`, §4; `earnings/08_earnings-red-flags.md`, §1 (Contradictions Between Agents) |

**RF-DISC-002 (recurring "one-off" / aggressive non-GAAP add-backs)** — triggered on the "legal, non-income tax, and regulatory reserve changes and settlements" line: $1,123M (FY2024) and $564M (FY2025), both years disclosed, both material, both framed as an "adjustment" despite recurring. Severity: High (materiality: the two-year add-back total, $1.687bn, is 3.2% of FY2025 revenue and 19.3% of FY2025 Adjusted EBITDA — well above the module's Medium/High related-item thresholds by analogy).

## 3. Disclosure Transparency

Disclosure got **less transparent, not more, over the period covered by this pool** — the single biggest finding in this report. Through FY2025, Uber disclosed a full GAAP-to-Adjusted-EBITDA reconciliation table in its 10-K, itemizing every add-back (SBC, D&A, legal/regulatory reserves, restructuring, impairments) [`earnings/06_earnings-quality.md`, §4, §7]. Beginning Q1 FY2026, that consolidated reconciliation vanished from the 10-Q body entirely, and the segment-level "Segment Adjusted EBITDA" measure was replaced with "Segment Operating Income" [Q1 FY26 10-Q; Q2 FY26 10-Q: "Beginning in the first quarter of 2026, we changed our segment operating performance measure from Segment Adjusted EBITDA to Segment Operating Income"]. The term "EBITDA" does not appear anywhere in either FY2026 10-Q outside that one methodology sentence, and does not appear in either FY2026 earnings-call transcript [`earnings/01_historical-financials.md`, §4]. Yet Uber continues to guide and report quantified beats on an "EBITDA" line through the Capital IQ guidance/consensus channel — beating the guided high end by ~2.5% in both Q1 and Q2 FY2026 [`earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md`, §6] — a claim that can no longer be verified against a primary-source GAAP reconciliation, because the company stopped publishing one. `earnings/08_earnings-red-flags.md` independently found it cannot bridge the $2,085M GAAP-basis EBITDA figure derivable from the FY2026 10-Q to the $2,819M "EBITDA" figure Uber is credited with beating, a $734M (35.2%) unexplained gap, precisely because the reconciliation is gone [`earnings/08_earnings-red-flags.md`, §1]. Separately, segment economics are disclosed at a reasonable level (Mobility, Delivery, Freight operating income and margin are all broken out), and the take-rate mechanics behind the UK Mobility change were explained with real precision in the Q2 Q&A (see §1). The litigation-naming gap described in §1 is the second transparency concern: a nationwide MDL with two adverse jury verdicts is not named in the "material legal proceedings" list of three consecutive filings, while smaller matters are named specifically.

**RF-DISC-001 (commentary contradicting the numbers)** — triggered. Management (via the guidance process and Capital IQ consensus channel) continues to make specific, quantified "beat guidance" claims on an EBITDA-type metric whose GAAP reconciliation the company itself stopped publishing starting Q1 FY2026 — the commentary about that metric can no longer be checked against a primary-source bridge to GAAP. Severity: High (materiality: this is the company's single most-cited profitability metric, used in every guidance cycle and in the promise-vs-delivery record scored in `01_management-and-track-record.md`).

**RF-REG-002 (delayed results / material-disclosure timeliness)** — triggered. The Arizona jury verdict ($8.5M, Feb-06-2026) landed one week before the FY25 10-K was filed (Feb-13-2026) and is not named in that filing's Legal Proceedings item; the Charlotte verdict ($5,000, Apr-20-2026) landed before the Q1 FY26 10-Q (filed May-06-2026) and is likewise not named there, nor in the subsequent Q2 FY26 10-Q. Severity: Medium (the aggregate legal reserve figure is disclosed and each individual verdict is small relative to the $1.8–2.1bn reserve, so this is a naming/specificity gap rather than a concealment of financial exposure).

## 4. Tone in Bad Times

Neither available call covers a genuine "bad quarter" — both Q1 and Q2 FY2026 beat Uber's own Adjusted-EBITDA-type guidance above the high end, so the sharpest test (owning an actual guided miss) cannot be run from this pool; that is a real limit on this section's conclusions, not glossed over. Prepared remarks in both calls are consistently promotional in register: Q2 opened with "Q2 was another outstanding quarter for Uber with record audience and engagement... record cash generation," and the pending Delivery Hero deal was framed with "we believe this deal will create significant long-term shareholder value" — a forward claim with no delivered evidence yet (the deal has not closed) [Q2 FY26 transcript, prepared remarks]. On the one occasion a headline number looked negative on its face — the ~500bps mobility take-rate decline — the CFO answered with an exact, checkable attribution (400 of 500bps mechanical/UK, not a real margin deterioration) rather than a vague "headwind" [Q2 FY26 transcript, Q&A]. That is a real, positive, specific data point on directness. Litigation matters (the MDL verdicts) were not raised by any analyst and were not volunteered by management on either call — an absence, not an evasive answer to a direct question, so it cannot be scored as Q&A deflection, but it does mean the calls contain zero acknowledgment of the company's highest-profile ongoing legal risk in the period covered.

## 5. Candor Read

Management is specific and quantitatively direct when questioned on operating mechanics it chooses to discuss — the CFO's exact-basis-point walk of the UK take-rate "decline" is a real candor signal, not boilerplate, and the CFO-transition statement ruling out accounting disputes has held up against the subsequent record. Set against that: the company discontinued its own GAAP-to-Adjusted-EBITDA reconciliation in FY2026 even as it kept claiming quantified guidance beats on that exact metric, a "one-off" legal/regulatory non-GAAP add-back has recurred at material size for two straight years, and a nationwide sexual-assault MDL with two adverse jury verdicts is absent from the named legal proceedings in three consecutive filings while smaller matters are named specifically. None of this is fabrication — every hard number checked against a primary filing was there to be checked, and the aggregate legal reserve is disclosed — but the pattern is one of selective specificity: precise and quantified on metrics management wants to explain, and quietly less verifiable or less named on metrics and matters it does not. **Verdict: mixed.** The single biggest candor signal is the vanished EBITDA reconciliation — a company cannot be graded "candid" for beating its own guidance on a profitability metric it has simultaneously made harder for outside readers to independently verify from the primary filing. This module found no unresolved adverse integrity signal about current senior management (per `01_management-and-track-record.md` §4A and `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` §3, which cleared the routed "buzz" as legacy, pre-Khosrowshahi conduct) — the disclosure concerns in this report are about candor and completeness of current-period reporting, not about executive integrity, and are scored on that narrower basis.

RF-DISC-001 (commentary contradicting the numbers)
RF-DISC-002 (recurring "one-off" / aggressive non-GAAP add-backs)
RF-REG-002 (delayed results / material-disclosure timeliness)
